---

Eli knows the man by smell.

Antiseptic soap. Hot plastic from an oxygen line. Something green and bitter
crushed in water.

The memory has no room around it. A ceiling moving above him. Somebody saying
*stay with me* in a language his body understood before his mind did. Pain
behind his left eye. A pair of hands keeping his head from turning toward a
door where people were shouting.

Somchai Vichai comes out of the treatment bay looking older than the memory and
more tired than anyone Eli has ever imagined inside a miracle.

He walks past Eli.

"She lives?" Sora asks.

"For now. The bleeding has slowed. She still needs a surgeon."

"You healed her."

"I stabilized her."

"I saw the field."

"Then you saw too much."

Somchai points to the waiting room. "Sit. All of you."

They sit.

Wren chooses the chair nearest the exterior door. Sora remains standing until
the clinic nurse tells her the shoulder dressing is leaking, then sits with
visible resentment. Eli studies the building's patched medical equipment the
way he studies any room that has to keep running on what it already has.

Eli watches Somchai.

The familiarity will not become a fact.

Somchai notices.

"What?"

"Have we met?"

"Many people meet nurses."

"After an attack. I was a child."

Somchai's face does not change quickly enough.

Eli touches the ridge behind his left ear.

"You held my head."

Somchai sits opposite him.

"What name did they use?"

"Babu."

The waiting room contracts.

Somchai looks at the scar, then at Eli's hands, then at the way he keeps his
left side angled from the window.

"You were smaller," he says.

It is a poor line. That makes Eli believe it.

"You knew my parents?"

"No."

"You were there."

"After."

"Who brought me?"

"Two people from a route I did not know. One had blood on both sleeves and none
of it was his. They said keep you breathing until the south handoff."

"Where?"

"A clinic that does not exist now."

"Who took me?"

"A woman with a burn on her hand. She gave me no name."

"Where did they go?"

"I don't know."

Each answer is a closed door. Eli feels the old impulse to make usefulness into
leverage.

"You lost me."

Somchai receives the accusation without adopting it.

"The route separated us."

"Did you look?"

"Yes."

"How long?"

"Long enough to damage other work. Not long enough to find you."

Plain. No apology shaped to make Eli comfort him.

"I remembered your soap."

Somchai looks down at his hands.

"Bad soap."

"It stayed."

"The body keeps cheap things."

For a moment neither knows what the reunion should require.

Somchai glances at the curtain, then back. "May I?"

"May you what?"

"Read you the way I read a patient when instruments are lying. Not a scan they
can file. Depth. Consent first."

Eli almost refuses on instinct. Then he thinks of the annex machine that made
obedience feel like the body's own conclusion.

"Yes."

Somchai does not touch his head. Two fingers rest lightly at Eli's wrist, the
way a nurse checks a pulse before asking anything expensive.

The room does not change. Eli does.

"You are loud inside," Somchai says, quiet enough that Wren need not hear.
"Most people are quiet. The old injury is scar. The receiver was always you."

Somchai does not take his fingers away yet.

"Look at the room."

Eli looks. Wren at the exterior door: he gets heat and exit-count, nothing
under it he can name. The nurse at the desk: almost nothing. Two pilgrims
asleep in plastic chairs: breath, pain, no second surface. Pin at the
refrigerator, insulin box in both hands: a small pressure like Eli's own, held
badly.

"Also loud," Somchai says. "He already knows. He is checking the insulin."

Somchai withdraws his hand.

Eli keeps looking at the quiet chairs. The work he thought everyone did is
only his, and Pin's, and a few others. He does not ask for a word for that.

Then he remembers the woman behind the curtain.

"She has Hassan's transfer category."

"She has a name."

"Aylin."

"Good."

"Can I speak to her?"

"No."

"When she wakes."

"If she asks."

"She may know where they moved him."

"That does not make waking safer."

"We are losing the convoy."

"Then lose it."

The words strike harder because Somchai has already saved Eli once.

"You don't understand."

"I understand urgency. It does not acquire patients."

"I am not trying to acquire her."

"You entered my clinic asking what she carries."

Eli looks toward the curtain.

He did.

Wren does not rescue him from the recognition.

"What may we do?" she asks.

Somchai answers her.

"Wait. Arrange blood that does not pass through security intake. Find a surgeon
who will come here. Keep the people outside from turning the waiting room into a
shrine. If Aylin wakes, someone who is not asking for her route tells her where
she is."

"And after?"

"She decides."

Sora says, "Security will reach the clinic."

"Yes."

"Because you treated her."

"Because they injured her."

"They will not preserve the distinction."

"Then help me move the other patients."

Sora goes to the medicine refrigerator with Pin.

Wren takes the exterior door and starts calling surgeons from there, one eye
on the road.

Eli waits.

It is the first task Somchai gives him.

---

Aylin wakes after sunset.

She does not ask for Eli.

She asks for water, the time, whether her wrist is still attached, and the
woman with the open collar.

Sora enters alone.

They speak for eleven minutes. Eli hears no words through the curtain, only
pauses and once the scrape of a cup.

When Sora comes out, she does not report.

"Well?" Eli asks.

"She wants Wren."

Wren goes in.

Another nine minutes.

When Wren returns, she holds Aylin's consent recorded on the clinic slate.

"She will tell us what she saw with Somchai present. No instruments. No field
read. No questions about anything before the holding route. She may stop."

Eli enters under those terms.

Aylin looks smaller awake. Her broken wrist rests on a splint. Somchai sits
beside the monitor, not touching her.

"Hassan is alive," she says.

The room loses air.

"You saw him?" Eli asks.

"Yes."

"Where?" Eli asks.

"East of the water. Then north through the passes."

She pauses. The collar at her throat pulses once—not field, just the memory of field—and she shifts her weight before she gives him the next piece.

"Six days." Her eyes hold his. "The depot they used is called Maltepe. I am not sure the name is right. If I am wrong, do not use it."

"Was Hassan in the convoy today?"

"No."

The answer kills a pursuit Eli has been continuing in his body since the hill.

"Why did the convoy carry his category?"

"We all carried it. Failed maps. People who read wrong. People the grounds
answered badly."

"What happened at Göbekli?"

Aylin's eyes move to Somchai.

"Do you want to stop?" he asks.

"No."

She looks back at Eli.

"They used the consortium clock to hold us quiet. It slipped. I hit the wall.
"The woman in front told me the rear lock would open if the system chose a
cleaner load."

Somchai's hand stays on the table. He does not look at Aylin. He looks at
Eli's wrist, where the pulse is faster than it should be.

"Anika?"

"She did not give her name."

Sora says nothing.

"Why throw you out?"

"Because my bleed was changing the rhythm."

The sentence contains the whole institution. A dying person became noisy
cargo.

"Hassan," Eli says. "Which depot?"

Aylin closes her eyes.

Somchai leans forward. "Stop."

"She hasn't said stop."

"Her body has."

"One name."

"Out."

Eli remains seated.

Somchai meets his eyes.

No force. No miracle. A nurse in a chair beside a patient.

"Out, Babu."

The childhood name removes every defense.

Eli leaves.

---

By midnight there are fourteen people outside the clinic.

The video has traveled faster than the ambulance.

Some bring records. Some bring children. One brings flowers wrapped in a
hospital discharge sheet. Two men stand apart and photograph everyone who
enters. A woman has written **HEALER** on cardboard in three languages.

On the corridor wall beside the old restaurant menu board, someone has chalked
a half-line in three languages and left it unfinished:

*I alone—*

Beside it, same white: Dei. Dio. Dievas.

Nobody explains it. Somchai wipes the wall, then tears the cardboard
**HEALER** in half.

A woman near the flowers, not quietly: "Two lights on a mast is rescue. One is
the sister who sinks you." She looks from Somchai to Eli when she says two.

Somchai does not look up.

"That seems unfriendly," says a man beside Eli.

He is burned along one side of his face. Not fresh. The scar pulls his mouth
into an expression that looks skeptical even when his voice is gentle. He
carries no visible weapon and wears the kind of ordinary dark coat institutions
use when they want danger to appear professional.

"Do you know him?" Eli asks.

"No. I know the need."

Outside, Somchai points the families toward clinic intake and sends everyone
else away. Several obey. Most do not.

"People kneel when standing has failed them," the scarred man says.

"And then?"

"Someone should help them stand."

"Somchai is trying."

"One patient at a time."

"That's how patients work."

The man smiles.

"You are Eli Holt."

Wren has already moved within reach.

"Who are you?" Eli asks.

"Sol Méndez."

Sora, across the waiting room, goes rigid.

Sol laughs, then stops when he sees her face.

"Sorry," he says. "Bad timing. I do that."

He holds out a paper bag. Candied ginger. Wren does not take it. He looks
offended for a second, then eats one himself.

"I am not here to threaten your clinic," he says, too loud for the room.
"Though I can see how it sounds."

He looks through the window at Somchai, who is examining a child's records
under the exterior light while refusing every hand that reaches for his sleeve.

"You and he are going to learn the same lesson," Sol says. "A gift this large
cannot remain private without becoming cruelty. I know, I know. Making it
public may become something worse. It bothers me. I want company anyway."

Wren opens the door.

"Conversation is over."

Sol talks a little past the close, then catches himself.

"The Thrace depot changes guard at dawn," he says. "Your archive copies were
useful."

"How did you get them?"

"Witnessed access."

Nadiya's cost, arriving.

Sol leaves before Eli can decide whether the information was help, bait, or
proof of surveillance.

Outside, nobody kneels to him.

Not yet.

Somchai comes in after the last child is assessed. He lowers himself into a
plastic chair and closes his eyes.

"Security arrives before dawn," Sora tells him.

"I know."

"You need a route."

"My patients need routes."

"The clinic is exposed."

Somchai opens his eyes.

"Then we move the clinic."

Not *I will join you*. Not *take me west*. The work first, carrying the man only
because the man belongs to it.

Eli looks through the curtain toward Aylin.

Hassan is alive. A depot exists. A guard changes at dawn.

Every answer has arrived through someone else's terms.

For once, none of those terms feels like delay.

---

Before the ambulance leaves, Somchai changes Sora's dressing himself. The shoulder burn has blistered into a ring. He cleans it with saline that smells like the sea. Sora watches his hands. They are steady in a way that does not reassure her — it means he has done this enough times to know exactly what he is looking at.

Eli's thumb cut has stiffened shut. He cannot bend the joint without pain. He wraps the borrowed glove over it and does not think about what that means for the work ahead.

Wren's boots are caked with Thrace mud. She scrapes them on the clinic step and leaves a line of brown behind. The mud is not symbolic. It is just mud. But the route ledger in her pocket has become a map of where they have been, and every new layer of dirt is a country they cannot revisit.

---

## Chapter 8—The Healer's Clinic [PLACEHOLDER]
---

The holding site air tastes of disinfectant and animal bedding. Sora's shoulder dressing is already damp at the edges. She changes it in the supply room while Somchai watches the door. The new bandage is tighter. She does not wince.

Eli's cut palm has swollen shut. He cannot flex the fingers far enough to hold a pen. He uses his left hand for everything until Somchai hands him a pencil and says, 'The right will work or it won't. Don't choose.'

Wren's boots still carry the Thrace mud inside the soles. She scrapes them on the clinic threshold before entering the supply room, but the mud does not come off. It has dried into the tread.

---

## Chapter 9—The Holding Entry [SPLIT-FROM: Ch 5]
The holding site used to inspect livestock.

This is how Sora describes it while Pin changes the dressing on her shoulder.

"Two vehicle gates. Wash-down floor. Individual pens along the east wall.
Administrative rooms above. Air handling separate by bay."

"How many guards?" Wren asks.

"Depends whether they still classify the people as research or transport."

"Which has more?"

"Research."

"And Hassan?"

"Both."

They are in the clinic supply room because it is the only place without a
patient. Cardboard cartons rise around them: dressings, saline, powdered milk,
antibiotics that expire next month and will not survive being moved twice.
Outside, the first families from the healer video are already returning despite
the dark.

Somchai has given them fifteen minutes. He does not give them a clock. What he
gives them instead is a chipped enamel mug sitting on the windowsill beside a
teabag still in its foil—something he pours for himself before every hard
conversation, the same measure he used to set on the counter for Wren's mother
when she came off a long placement run. He drinks it standing, half the cup,
then sets it down empty before he will discuss the clinic's evacuation, not a
rescue.

Wren draws the holding site on the back of a medication inventory.

"Why keep a research subject in a transfer pen?"

"His memory does not respond consistently to extraction," Sora says. "They move
him between teams. Each team assumes the previous method contaminated him."

"Did it?"

"He lies."

Eli feels an unreasonable flare of pride.

"Well?"

"Very well."

Eli sits on an oxygen carton. "He has my father's ring."

"You keep saying that like it increases his value," Wren says.

"It increases what they will do to him."

The correction lands.

Somchai comes in carrying a tray of unused medications from bay three.

"Time."

"We know where Hassan is," Eli says.

"Good."

"The guard changes at dawn."

"Also good."

Before Eli can start on the depot timing, Wren holds up a hand. She has been
looking at the medication tray, not the route.

"Three of these are expired," she says. "The lot numbers match the batch the
pharmacy recalled last month."

Somchai checks the tray without surprise. "I was told the replacement arrived."

"It did. The shelves here weren't updated."

Somchai picks up the expired blister pack. For a moment he holds it the way he
holds a body he cannot save: not with hope, not with anger, with the quiet
fact that someone was promised care and given paperwork instead.

"These were allocated to the convoy clinic three weeks ago," he says. "The
pharmacy replaced them on paper. The shelf kept the old lot. The paper is the
thing that traveled."

Eli thinks of Hassan's transfer category—paper moving faster than a man. The
same machinery in a different register. One moves a prisoner through categories
that pretend he does not exist; the other moves medicine through categories that
pretend it is current. Both rely on the assumption that the next person in the
chain will not look. Somchai is looking.

"We will not use them," Somchai says. He places the tray on the medication
counter, face up, so the expired lot numbers show. "Call the regional pharmacy
and tell them the replacement they think they delivered arrived with a review
date already past. They will argue. Tell them the argument will be forwarded to
the clinic board with the nurse's name attached."

Wren looks at him.

Somchai does not look back. "I am not the person who heals on command. I am the
person who says no in public when the cost is small enough that no one will
remember my name afterward. That is the hard no, not the moment with cameras."

Eli opens his mouth to frame it — the crowd needs a symbol, the clinic needs
cover — but Somchai answers before he speaks.

"You are reading the room wrong," Somchai says. "The woman with the burned
face is not afraid of me. She is reading my body. The patient behind the
curtain does not want a healer. She wants someone who will not decide for her.
You called this a clinic. It is a place where people come to be asked."

Eli listens. The file he was about to make — healer-as-instrument — does not
survive the sentence.

Somchai picks up the unexpired pack and puts it into the tray before anyone else
can. "Now we plan the clinic evacuation."

Eli files the sentence the way he files cheap soap and ordinary grief: under
things that should not need saving and do anyway.

He does not argue. He marks the three vials with a red pen from his pocket and
puts them in a separate tray, without a word, without correcting anyone. The
team is already moving; this is caught without breaking stride.

Eli starts on the depot timing. Wren is already two steps ahead of him on the
medication recall.

The compact does not work because Eli is the one who sees everything. It works
because Wren sees what Eli misses, and she does not make him thank her for it.

"Wait," he says. "There's a window between shift change and decontamination. The system clears the outgoing team's return credentials before it loads the incoming team's checks. We have ninety seconds where the gate is green for anybody carrying a medical transponder that was just cleared out."

Somchai looks at him. "Where did you learn that?"

"Pelangi. I watched them load and unload the same corridor. The Mandate trusts the exit more than the entry. It's faster to believe somebody already left than to verify somebody just arrived."

Wren studies the transfer file, then Eli's face. "He's right."

Eli hears the route close.

"What do we take?"

Wren points through the wall toward the crowd outside.

"Attention."

---

By four in the morning, fifty-seven people are waiting for Somchai.

The video has acquired music, captions, a false location, and a second healing
he did not perform. A feed claims the nurse raised a dead prisoner at Göbekli.
Another identifies him as one of two sons of a sky god whose name Eli has seen
only in damaged comparative notes.

Somchai does not read either.

He sets up triage tables under work lights and begins with the people who have
appointments.

This makes the crowd furious.

It also makes the clinic visible enough that every security feed in the
corridor turns toward it.

Sora leaves through the medicine-loading door in a laundry truck borrowed from
the restaurant next door. Eli lies between bags of sheets. Wren rides
in front beside Pin, who is driving because he knows the service roads and
because guilt has made him generous in ways nobody trusts.

"You understand this is not forgiveness," Somchai told him.

"I understand you keep saying that."

"Then perhaps one day you will."

Somchai remains with the patients.

The ambulance departs in the opposite direction carrying Aylin, two surgical
patients, and a physician who agreed to meet them beyond the regional security
cordon after Nadiya called every archive trustee who owed her a favor.

No patient becomes cover.

At the first junction, Pin removes the laundry truck's clinic magnet and throws
it into a drainage ditch.

"That was useful," Eli says.

"That was stolen."

"From whom?"

"Somchai."

"He knew?"

"He knows everything I do five minutes after it becomes expensive."

They cross west under a sky beginning to pale.

The holding site lies beyond a freight yard where the old animal road meets the
new security corridor. Concrete walls. Curved roof. A line of wash arches large
enough for cattle trucks. No sign names the people inside.

Sora sits up.

"From here, do exactly what I say."

"You defected," Wren says.

"The gate does not know."

She closes her collar.

Pain crosses her face before the seam locks.

The person who emerged at Sanxingdui disappears behind posture: shoulders
level, eyes emptied of objection, one hand held where a weapon should be.

Eli looks at her and sees Hassan's transfer category in another register: a
person made to carry a shape that is not her own until the thing she is carrying
stops being useful. He thinks of the pen twelve description Sora gave them—cattle
trucks, wash arches, concrete walls. He thinks of the guard changing at dawn,
which means the night shift is already tired, already watching the perimeter
instead of the pens, already making the small mistakes a rescue depends on.

He does not say this aloud. He does not need to. Wren is already routing the
night-shift fatigue data through a house in Thrace that does not know her name.

At the service gate she presents herself as Shield Vithan returning contaminated
materials from the Göbekli incident.

The guard asks for the materials.

Sora points at the laundry bay.

"Neurological textiles."

The guard recoils from the truck without knowing why.

The gate opens.

Wren feels the exit the way she feels a route closing: before the wheels turn.
She has not told Eli half of what she arranged inside the holding site. The bribe
to the gate clerk was routed through a house in Albion that does not know her
name. The uniform in the cab is a modified Thread issue that would identify her
as a Weaver if anyone checked the weave. The guard's recoil was engineered by a
field signature borrowed from Sora's open collar and broadcast through the
truck's cab fan.
Every step was rehearsed in rooms Eli was not invited to attend. Hub labor has
no signature. It appears as the thing already moving when someone finally asks
who started it.

Inside the cab, Sora does not speak.

Inside, cleanliness becomes threat.

White floor sloped to drains. White pens with doors that seal at the bottom.
White observation windows polarized against the people behind them. The air
smells of disinfectant and the older animal fear no renovation removed.

Pin parks beside the laundry intake.

Sora gives them ninety seconds.

Wren stays with the truck. This is not her network, her credential, or her
operation. She keeps the exit alive and refuses the temptation to make
unfamiliar systems look like competence.

Eli follows Sora through the wash corridor.

At the first interior arch, the collar reads Sora.

**SHIELD / ACTIVE SAFEGUARD / RETURN AUTHORIZED.**

The system asks her to surrender contaminated companions for assessment.

"Override," she says.

**REASON.**

"Command exposure."

The arch turns green.

Eli watches her. "Whose command?"

"Mine."

They pass.

Hassan is in pen twelve.

Eli knows him first by the way he sits: forward on the bench, elbows on knees,
as if an ordinary conversation has been delayed and he intends to charge
somebody for the time. His hair has gone gray at one temple since Malacca. A
healed split crosses his lower lip.

He looks at Eli through the glass.

"You took your time."

The voice enters like a piece of the night market preserved under ice.

Eli puts his palm to the door.

"Can you stand?"

"Can. Won't until you open it."

Sora uses her collar at the lock.

Red.

**SUBJECT UNDER EVIDENTIARY HOLD. DUAL AUTHORIZATION REQUIRED.**

"Who is second?" Eli asks.

A voice answers from the ceiling.

"Me."

Anika appears on the observation glass.

Behind the glass, a woman in clinic gray is trying to push a medication cart
through the administrative door. The door has inherited the same evidentiary
lock as the pens.

"Captain. Clinical override for twelve," she says.

Anika does not look away from Sora. "Hold, Devika."

"His anticonvulsant is due in eleven minutes. Nine and fourteen are due now."

"Security event."

Devika Sen braces one shoe against the cart wheel. Her badge says **CLINIC
ADMINISTRATION**. A smaller line carries the Charkha grant code.

"Then secure the corridor. The medication still goes through."

Sora's jaw tightens. "Charkha," she says, mostly to herself, the way you name
a weather system already overhead.

She is live, not recorded — the scar beside her mouth tightens when she sees
Sora's closed collar.

"You returned," Anika says.

"Open twelve."

"You know I cannot."

"You can."

"Then you know why I will not."

The glass beside pen twelve clears.

An administrative room sits beyond it. On one table is a black data case. On
another, under transparent evidence film, lies a small cloth packet Eli
recognizes.

Maren's field notebook fragment.

Wren carried it from Malacca to Sanxingdui. It was in her pack at Göbekli.

Eli turns toward the corridor camera.

"Wren."

Her voice comes through the earpiece. "Still here."

"Check your pack."

A pause.

Then: "Copy is gone."

Not the physical wafer. A mirrored fragment lifted through one of the witnessed
archive exchanges, perhaps. Enough to make the packet look authorized.

Anika opens the data case remotely.

A screen wakes.

Maren sits in a clinical room, older than every memory Eli carries. Her hair is
cut at the jaw. Electrodes cross one temple. She is arguing with someone beyond
the camera.

No sound.

Anika says, "The clean file contains nineteen hours. Initial classifier design.
Subject-selection objections. Sabotage sequence. Current western placement."

"Current?"

"Verified forty-one hours ago."

Eli leans toward the screen. His hand lifts to the frame as if to verify a
face by touch. He stops himself.

"Timestamp hash."

Anika does not provide one.

"Origin routing?"

"Classified under active transfer protocols."

He accepts both answers without pressing. The strip in his shirt knows the
shape of verification; his hands have stopped listening to it.

Maren alive now. Not two winters. Not a correction surviving its writer.

Now.

"Open it."

"Return Maren's field notebook key and Sora. The file releases to you. Mr.
Hassan continues under witnessed protection."

Hassan laughs inside the pen.

"Protection has drains."

Eli does not step toward the evidence room. He wants to.

"Forty-one hours?"

"Yes, Mr. Holt."

"Show a timestamp."

Anika does.

His breath leaves him.

On the silent screen, Maren lifts one hand in irritation. The gesture is so
ordinary he almost breaks. She used the same hand to stop Rasel correcting her
while she counted water into the garden beds.

"Where?" Eli asks.

"The file."

"Give me the location."

"The file is the offer."

Sora looks at pen twelve, then at the evidence room.

"There will be a purge when the shift changes."

Anika's face remains composed. "There will be a transfer."

"Same drains."

Devika strikes the clinic-door release with the heel of her hand. "Evidence can
wait outside my medication room."

The lock stays red.

She calls maintenance, gives the grant code as billing authorization, and tells
them three doses are late.

"You are injured and command-exposed. Your threat assessment is unreliable."

"Your model said I would return alone."

For the first time Anika's gaze moves away.

Small miss.

Sora removes the medal from beneath her collar. Michael's small metal face.
She wedges it into the emergency seam of pen twelve.

"That is not a key," Eli says.

"No."

She strikes it with the base of her palm.

The lock shorts.

Every door in the corridor seals.

Alarm.

"That," Sora says, "is a key."

---

The evidence room opens automatically during fire isolation.

Pen twelve does not.

Eli is at Hassan's door. The black case sits open across the corridor, copying
without anyone to watch it.

Through the glass, Hassan raises one eyebrow.

"Mother or witness," he says. "They learned you."

"Quiet."

"I was quiet for months."

The emergency seam has exposed three wires. Sora names them by color. Eli uses
the ceramic wedge from Göbekli to bridge the two she indicates.

The door unlocks one centimeter.

Hassan gets his fingers into the gap from inside.

Across the corridor the screen begins copying Maren's file into the open case.

**NINETEEN HOURS.**

**ESTIMATED TRANSFER: SIX MINUTES.**

Boots strike the far wash corridor.

Wren says, "Exit loses in eighty seconds."

Eli watches the progress bar. The placement block will close last. He knows
that without anyone saying it. Metadata always does.

Hassan and Eli pull the door.

It moves another centimeter.


holding every contact in one state. Eli can feel its source in the evidence
room.

The file transfer and the prison lock share a clock.

Of course they do.

Evidence or person.

One system, making the choice efficient.

He could cross to the case. Forty seconds of watching would not open Hassan's
door. Six minutes of watching would not fit Wren's eighty.

Hassan is here.

Maren is there.

On the screen, the silent recording reaches a new segment.

Maren stands over a projection of human signatures. Younger. Uninjured. She
selects a clustering method and watches the system sort people into colored
groups.

Not captive.

Building it.

Eli stares.

Anika gives the recording sound.

"The false-positive rate is acceptable if the intervention remains voluntary,"
Maren says. "We are identifying people who need shielding before overload, not
assigning legal identity."

Someone beyond frame asks what prevents later use.

"Governance."

The word from his mother's mouth hurts more than a confession.

The recording jumps years.

Maren is older, wired at the temple, correcting a field model.

"If you collapse the divergence, bay four arrests," she says. "Leave the phase
error. I don't care what the central trace looks like. Those are people, not
noise."

Voluntary correction.

Keeping captives alive inside the apparatus she helped make possible.

Neither innocence nor betrayal. Conduct under a door she built before somebody
locked it.

The transfer reaches nine percent.

Wren says, "Thirty seconds."

Eli pulls the copy cable from the case.

The copy breaks.

The prison lock loses its clock.

Hassan's door opens.

The broken bar sits at nine percent. He does not look at it again.

"Move," Eli says.

Hassan steps into the corridor and nearly falls.

Eli's hand is out before his mind catches up to it — the same reach, the same angle, as a rooftop in Malacca where this man's fist wouldn't open around a ring worth more than his own freedom. The van doors closed on both of them that day. These doors are opening instead.

Sora takes his weight only after he nods.

The guards reach the inner arch.

Eli pockets the recorder containing nine percent of his mother.

They run.

---

Wren has moved the laundry truck through the wash bay and reversed it against
the emergency doors, so the rear cargo box forms a narrow bridge across the
security vestibule.

"I said eighty seconds," she tells them as they climb through. "That was
eighty-six."

"File was large," Hassan says.

She sees him and stops being angry for exactly one breath.

"You're Hassan."

"Today."

Pin drives before the doors close.

Security rounds strike the truck's rear panel. Sora braces a linen cart against
it. Eli lies on the floor beside Hassan, the broken copy cable still in his
fist as if warmth might make the missing ninety-one percent return.

The truck clears the animal road.

No one speaks until the holding site disappears behind freight sheds.

Then Hassan reaches into his mouth.

He works loose a false lower tooth, spits blood into a towel, and breaks the
tooth casing open.

Inside is a ring.

Silver and brass. Imperfect seam. The metal dulled by years of saliva,
disinfectant, and hiding.

Hassan gives it to Eli.

"Your father would have asked. Your mother told me to give it to the one who
came."

Eli does not take it at first.

"You saw her?"

"Years ago. Then in a transfer room through glass. She recognized the
inscription."

"What did she say?"

"That he always made metaphors when engineering failed."

Eli takes the ring.

The band is too wide for his finger and too heavy to put away. He holds it in
his palm instead.

"Same soil," Hassan says. "She put the phrase into her word index. Said if the
maps were stripped, relation would still travel under ordinary language."

Eli touches the nine-percent slate in his pocket.

"She's alive."

"Yes."

"Where?"

Hassan leans his head against the laundry sacks.

"West."

"I know west."

"Then know more carefully."

"Albion?"

Hassan's eyes open.

"They stopped calling it Britain after the crown stopped mattering. Your mother
called it Albion before the files did."

Eli closes his hand around the ring.

"Site."

"No address. They move the administrative layer. The ground stays. She is part
of the stabilization mesh now."

"Wired in," Sora says.

"Kept alive because the damaged western network only agrees when she corrects
it."

Eli sees the nine staff on the Sanxingdui stair. A central operator smoothing
disagreement until living traces appear as one answer.

"Can she leave?"

Hassan looks at him.

"You keep asking rescue questions."

"What other kind is there?"

"The kind where removing her changes what everyone else survives."

The truck crosses a bridge. Gray water appears between freight buildings and is
gone.

"Her signal stabilizes old grounds from the Balkans to Albion," Hassan says.
"It also helps them locate people those grounds answer. Prison and lighthouse.
Cut her out, you lose both."

"She wants out."

"She wanted many things each time I saw her."

"Did she ask you?"

"She said if her son came, tell him a route completed on paper is not a person
returned."

Wren looks back from the front seat.

The line does not belong to her yet. It strikes anyway.

"What else?" Eli asks.

"She said not to let you make her innocent because you needed a mother."

The ring shifts in Eli's palm.

"There was a name," Hassan adds.

He turns it over the way he used to turn route slips in the depot kitchen—edge
to edge, twice, checking the handwriting before anyone else did. "Early program.
Subject she defended as temporary hold—signature model, humane framing. Delay so
she could reform the room from inside. The delay acquired a person."

"Who?"

"Imani Vale. Longitudinal trial. She asked to leave. Your mother delayed the
withdrawal because the data completed a cohort—temporary, humane framing.
Lang's office used the instability from the delay as proof she needed
protection. She never left. Your mother told me the name herself through the
glass. Said if you came asking for a saint, give you a ledger."

Eli closes his fist around the ring until the metal bites.

Eli files *Imani Vale* beside *alive* and *wired* and finds that mother
will not become a prize again without that name sitting in the room.

---

They stop after sunrise in an abandoned toll plaza where three roads have been
blocked by old flood barriers and a fourth remains open because local drivers
move the concrete every time authorities replace it.

Pin sleeps against the steering wheel.

Sora watches the road.

Eli sits beneath the toll canopy with the ring beside him, not on him. Wren
does not take it. She will hold it for evidence if he asks. He does not ask.

Hassan allows Wren to clean the split in his mouth. He tells his story into no
recorder. She listens anyway.

Eli reviews the nine percent of Maren's file.

The classifier segment. The bay-four correction. Forty-one-hour timestamp. At
the end, a damaged chart flashes for less than a second before the copy fails.
Blank wedges cut through central Europe. Around one blank, Maren has written:

**NOT ABSENCE. SCAR.**

Below it is a burial-site reference and a repeated registry mark.

Pömmelte.

A dead zone on the western route.

Eli rewinds to the first recording.

*The false-positive rate is acceptable if the intervention stays voluntary.*

Maren wrote that, then showed what happens when it does not. The classifier
that sent her bay-four correction also froze her corridor access for eleven
months. Rasel kept building the loops anyway. Eli thought finding either
parent would put the household back.

Wren sits beside him, not touching, close enough that he can feel warmth from
her arm.

He leans toward it before deciding what he is asking.

She moves away.

The loss is small after everything else. It reaches the same place.

"What?" he asks.

"Don't put her in my chair."

"I'm not."

"You found out your mother is alive, compromised, and may not come with you.
Now you want me to become the place that does."

"I wanted—"

He cannot finish without lying.

Wren waits.

"Home," he says.

"I know."

"Is that wrong?"

"No." She looks across the plaza toward the ring. "Making me responsible for
replacing it would be."

"I didn't ask you to."

"Your body did."

He thinks of Aylin's body stopping the interview before her mouth did.

Wren has spent six weeks mapping Mandate transfers from the inside. Each
window opens onto a smaller room than the last. She has not told Eli. She is
waiting for him to ask.

"I'm sorry."

"Don't be sorry for wanting me."

The sentence opens hope so quickly he almost misuses it.

"Then what?"

"Want me specifically."

She stands.

"Not because your mother left a chair."

Wren goes to relieve Sora at the road.

Eli remains beneath the toll canopy with the partial recording and the first
clean knowledge that his mother is alive.

It does not feel clean.

That may be the first honest thing about it.

---

The dawn bus to the holding site is half-empty. Eli sits with his right hand curled against his chest. The thumb cut has opened again. He presses a tissue to it and does not tie it. Tourniquets belong to people who have time to treat wounds properly, and they do not.

Sora's shoulder burns through the new dressing. She leans her weight against the window and watches the Elbe corridor rise from flat fields. The water is higher than the maps said. She notes this without speaking. Nothing they carry is waterproof.

Wren has not slept. She watches the corridor markers pass and calculates how far they have traveled since the container exited at the freight exchange. Thirty-one hours in the box, twelve on the road, four in the archive, six in the clinic. The arithmetic is not a comfort.

---

## Chapter 10—The Midpoint Revelation [SPLIT-FROM: Ch 5]
The holding site used to inspect livestock.

This is how Sora describes it while Pin changes the dressing on her shoulder.

"Two vehicle gates. Wash-down floor. Individual pens along the east wall.
Administrative rooms above. Air handling separate by bay."

"How many guards?" Wren asks.

"Depends whether they still classify the people as research or transport."

"Which has more?"

"Research."

"And Hassan?"

"Both."

They are in the clinic supply room because it is the only place without a
patient. Cardboard cartons rise around them: dressings, saline, powdered milk,
antibiotics that expire next month and will not survive being moved twice.
Outside, the first families from the healer video are already returning despite
the dark.

Somchai has given them fifteen minutes. He does not give them a clock. What he
gives them instead is a chipped enamel mug sitting on the windowsill beside a
teabag still in its foil—something he pours for himself before every hard
conversation, the same measure he used to set on the counter for Wren's mother
when she came off a long placement run. He drinks it standing, half the cup,
then sets it down empty before he will discuss the clinic's evacuation, not a
rescue.

Wren draws the holding site on the back of a medication inventory.

"Why keep a research subject in a transfer pen?"

"His memory does not respond consistently to extraction," Sora says. "They move
him between teams. Each team assumes the previous method contaminated him."

"Did it?"

"He lies."

Eli feels an unreasonable flare of pride.

"Well?"

"Very well."

Eli sits on an oxygen carton. "He has my father's ring."

"You keep saying that like it increases his value," Wren says.

"It increases what they will do to him."

The correction lands.

Somchai comes in carrying a tray of unused medications from bay three.

"Time."

"We know where Hassan is," Eli says.

"Good."

"The guard changes at dawn."

"Also good."

Before Eli can start on the depot timing, Wren holds up a hand. She has been
looking at the medication tray, not the route.

"Three of these are expired," she says. "The lot numbers match the batch the
pharmacy recalled last month."

Somchai checks the tray without surprise. "I was told the replacement arrived."

"It did. The shelves here weren't updated."

Somchai picks up the expired blister pack. For a moment he holds it the way he
holds a body he cannot save: not with hope, not with anger, with the quiet
fact that someone was promised care and given paperwork instead.

"These were allocated to the convoy clinic three weeks ago," he says. "The
pharmacy replaced them on paper. The shelf kept the old lot. The paper is the
thing that traveled."

Eli thinks of Hassan's transfer category—paper moving faster than a man. The
same machinery in a different register. One moves a prisoner through categories
that pretend he does not exist; the other moves medicine through categories that
pretend it is current. Both rely on the assumption that the next person in the
chain will not look. Somchai is looking.

"We will not use them," Somchai says. He places the tray on the medication
counter, face up, so the expired lot numbers show. "Call the regional pharmacy
and tell them the replacement they think they delivered arrived with a review
date already past. They will argue. Tell them the argument will be forwarded to
the clinic board with the nurse's name attached."

Wren looks at him.

Somchai does not look back. "I am not the person who heals on command. I am the
person who says no in public when the cost is small enough that no one will
remember my name afterward. That is the hard no, not the moment with cameras."

Eli opens his mouth to frame it — the crowd needs a symbol, the clinic needs
cover — but Somchai answers before he speaks.

"You are reading the room wrong," Somchai says. "The woman with the burned
face is not afraid of me. She is reading my body. The patient behind the
curtain does not want a healer. She wants someone who will not decide for her.
You called this a clinic. It is a place where people come to be asked."

Eli listens. The file he was about to make — healer-as-instrument — does not
survive the sentence.

Somchai picks up the unexpired pack and puts it into the tray before anyone else
can. "Now we plan the clinic evacuation."

Eli files the sentence the way he files cheap soap and ordinary grief: under
things that should not need saving and do anyway.

He does not argue. He marks the three vials with a red pen from his pocket and
puts them in a separate tray, without a word, without correcting anyone. The
team is already moving; this is caught without breaking stride.

Eli starts on the depot timing. Wren is already two steps ahead of him on the
medication recall.

The compact does not work because Eli is the one who sees everything. It works
because Wren sees what Eli misses, and she does not make him thank her for it.

"Wait," he says. "There's a window between shift change and decontamination. The system clears the outgoing team's return credentials before it loads the incoming team's checks. We have ninety seconds where the gate is green for anybody carrying a medical transponder that was just cleared out."

Somchai looks at him. "Where did you learn that?"

"Pelangi. I watched them load and unload the same corridor. The Mandate trusts the exit more than the entry. It's faster to believe somebody already left than to verify somebody just arrived."

Wren studies the transfer file, then Eli's face. "He's right."

Eli hears the route close.

"What do we take?"

Wren points through the wall toward the crowd outside.

"Attention."

---

By four in the morning, fifty-seven people are waiting for Somchai.

The video has acquired music, captions, a false location, and a second healing
he did not perform. A feed claims the nurse raised a dead prisoner at Göbekli.
Another identifies him as one of two sons of a sky god whose name Eli has seen
only in damaged comparative notes.

Somchai does not read either.

He sets up triage tables under work lights and begins with the people who have
appointments.

This makes the crowd furious.

It also makes the clinic visible enough that every security feed in the
corridor turns toward it.

Sora leaves through the medicine-loading door in a laundry truck borrowed from
the restaurant next door. Eli lies between bags of sheets. Wren rides
in front beside Pin, who is driving because he knows the service roads and
because guilt has made him generous in ways nobody trusts.

"You understand this is not forgiveness," Somchai told him.

"I understand you keep saying that."

"Then perhaps one day you will."

Somchai remains with the patients.

The ambulance departs in the opposite direction carrying Aylin, two surgical
patients, and a physician who agreed to meet them beyond the regional security
cordon after Nadiya called every archive trustee who owed her a favor.

No patient becomes cover.

At the first junction, Pin removes the laundry truck's clinic magnet and throws
it into a drainage ditch.

"That was useful," Eli says.

"That was stolen."

"From whom?"

"Somchai."

"He knew?"

"He knows everything I do five minutes after it becomes expensive."

They cross west under a sky beginning to pale.

The holding site lies beyond a freight yard where the old animal road meets the
new security corridor. Concrete walls. Curved roof. A line of wash arches large
enough for cattle trucks. No sign names the people inside.

Sora sits up.

"From here, do exactly what I say."

"You defected," Wren says.

"The gate does not know."

She closes her collar.

Pain crosses her face before the seam locks.

The person who emerged at Sanxingdui disappears behind posture: shoulders
level, eyes emptied of objection, one hand held where a weapon should be.

Eli looks at her and sees Hassan's transfer category in another register: a
person made to carry a shape that is not her own until the thing she is carrying
stops being useful. He thinks of the pen twelve description Sora gave them—cattle
trucks, wash arches, concrete walls. He thinks of the guard changing at dawn,
which means the night shift is already tired, already watching the perimeter
instead of the pens, already making the small mistakes a rescue depends on.

He does not say this aloud. He does not need to. Wren is already routing the
night-shift fatigue data through a house in Thrace that does not know her name.

At the service gate she presents herself as Shield Vithan returning contaminated
materials from the Göbekli incident.

The guard asks for the materials.

Sora points at the laundry bay.

"Neurological textiles."

The guard recoils from the truck without knowing why.

The gate opens.

Wren feels the exit the way she feels a route closing: before the wheels turn.
She has not told Eli half of what she arranged inside the holding site. The bribe
to the gate clerk was routed through a house in Albion that does not know her
name. The uniform in the cab is a modified Thread issue that would identify her
as a Weaver if anyone checked the weave. The guard's recoil was engineered by a
field signature borrowed from Sora's open collar and broadcast through the
truck's cab fan.
Every step was rehearsed in rooms Eli was not invited to attend. Hub labor has
no signature. It appears as the thing already moving when someone finally asks
who started it.

Inside the cab, Sora does not speak.

Inside, cleanliness becomes threat.

White floor sloped to drains. White pens with doors that seal at the bottom.
White observation windows polarized against the people behind them. The air
smells of disinfectant and the older animal fear no renovation removed.

Pin parks beside the laundry intake.

Sora gives them ninety seconds.

Wren stays with the truck. This is not her network, her credential, or her
operation. She keeps the exit alive and refuses the temptation to make
unfamiliar systems look like competence.

Eli follows Sora through the wash corridor.

At the first interior arch, the collar reads Sora.

**SHIELD / ACTIVE SAFEGUARD / RETURN AUTHORIZED.**

The system asks her to surrender contaminated companions for assessment.

"Override," she says.

**REASON.**

"Command exposure."

The arch turns green.

Eli watches her. "Whose command?"

"Mine."

They pass.

Hassan is in pen twelve.

Eli knows him first by the way he sits: forward on the bench, elbows on knees,
as if an ordinary conversation has been delayed and he intends to charge
somebody for the time. His hair has gone gray at one temple since Malacca. A
healed split crosses his lower lip.

He looks at Eli through the glass.

"You took your time."

The voice enters like a piece of the night market preserved under ice.

Eli puts his palm to the door.

"Can you stand?"

"Can. Won't until you open it."

Sora uses her collar at the lock.

Red.

**SUBJECT UNDER EVIDENTIARY HOLD. DUAL AUTHORIZATION REQUIRED.**

"Who is second?" Eli asks.

A voice answers from the ceiling.

"Me."

Anika appears on the observation glass.

Behind the glass, a woman in clinic gray is trying to push a medication cart
through the administrative door. The door has inherited the same evidentiary
lock as the pens.

"Captain. Clinical override for twelve," she says.

Anika does not look away from Sora. "Hold, Devika."

"His anticonvulsant is due in eleven minutes. Nine and fourteen are due now."

"Security event."

Devika Sen braces one shoe against the cart wheel. Her badge says **CLINIC
ADMINISTRATION**. A smaller line carries the Charkha grant code.

"Then secure the corridor. The medication still goes through."

Sora's jaw tightens. "Charkha," she says, mostly to herself, the way you name
a weather system already overhead.

She is live, not recorded — the scar beside her mouth tightens when she sees
Sora's closed collar.

"You returned," Anika says.

"Open twelve."

"You know I cannot."

"You can."

"Then you know why I will not."

The glass beside pen twelve clears.

An administrative room sits beyond it. On one table is a black data case. On
another, under transparent evidence film, lies a small cloth packet Eli
recognizes.

Maren's field notebook fragment.

Wren carried it from Malacca to Sanxingdui. It was in her pack at Göbekli.

Eli turns toward the corridor camera.

"Wren."

Her voice comes through the earpiece. "Still here."

"Check your pack."

A pause.

Then: "Copy is gone."

Not the physical wafer. A mirrored fragment lifted through one of the witnessed
archive exchanges, perhaps. Enough to make the packet look authorized.

Anika opens the data case remotely.

A screen wakes.

Maren sits in a clinical room, older than every memory Eli carries. Her hair is
cut at the jaw. Electrodes cross one temple. She is arguing with someone beyond
the camera.

No sound.

Anika says, "The clean file contains nineteen hours. Initial classifier design.
Subject-selection objections. Sabotage sequence. Current western placement."

"Current?"

"Verified forty-one hours ago."

Eli leans toward the screen. His hand lifts to the frame as if to verify a
face by touch. He stops himself.

"Timestamp hash."

Anika does not provide one.

"Origin routing?"

"Classified under active transfer protocols."

He accepts both answers without pressing. The strip in his shirt knows the
shape of verification; his hands have stopped listening to it.

Maren alive now. Not two winters. Not a correction surviving its writer.

Now.

"Open it."

"Return Maren's field notebook key and Sora. The file releases to you. Mr.
Hassan continues under witnessed protection."

Hassan laughs inside the pen.

"Protection has drains."

Eli does not step toward the evidence room. He wants to.

"Forty-one hours?"

"Yes, Mr. Holt."

"Show a timestamp."

Anika does.

His breath leaves him.

On the silent screen, Maren lifts one hand in irritation. The gesture is so
ordinary he almost breaks. She used the same hand to stop Rasel correcting her
while she counted water into the garden beds.

"Where?" Eli asks.

"The file."

"Give me the location."

"The file is the offer."

Sora looks at pen twelve, then at the evidence room.

"There will be a purge when the shift changes."

Anika's face remains composed. "There will be a transfer."

"Same drains."

Devika strikes the clinic-door release with the heel of her hand. "Evidence can
wait outside my medication room."

The lock stays red.

She calls maintenance, gives the grant code as billing authorization, and tells
them three doses are late.

"You are injured and command-exposed. Your threat assessment is unreliable."

"Your model said I would return alone."

For the first time Anika's gaze moves away.

Small miss.

Sora removes the medal from beneath her collar. Michael's small metal face.
She wedges it into the emergency seam of pen twelve.

"That is not a key," Eli says.

"No."

She strikes it with the base of her palm.

The lock shorts.

Every door in the corridor seals.

Alarm.

"That," Sora says, "is a key."

---

The evidence room opens automatically during fire isolation.

Pen twelve does not.

Eli is at Hassan's door. The black case sits open across the corridor, copying
without anyone to watch it.

Through the glass, Hassan raises one eyebrow.

"Mother or witness," he says. "They learned you."

"Quiet."

"I was quiet for months."

The emergency seam has exposed three wires. Sora names them by color. Eli uses
the ceramic wedge from Göbekli to bridge the two she indicates.

The door unlocks one centimeter.

Hassan gets his fingers into the gap from inside.

Across the corridor the screen begins copying Maren's file into the open case.

**NINETEEN HOURS.**

**ESTIMATED TRANSFER: SIX MINUTES.**

Boots strike the far wash corridor.

Wren says, "Exit loses in eighty seconds."

Eli watches the progress bar. The placement block will close last. He knows
that without anyone saying it. Metadata always does.

Hassan and Eli pull the door.

It moves another centimeter.

The field lock engages around the frame, not mechanical pressure but a rhythm
holding every contact in one state. Eli can feel its source in the evidence
room.

The file transfer and the prison lock share a clock.

Of course they do.

Evidence or person.

One system, making the choice efficient.

He could cross to the case. Forty seconds of watching would not open Hassan's
door. Six minutes of watching would not fit Wren's eighty.

Hassan is here.

Maren is there.

On the screen, the silent recording reaches a new segment.

Maren stands over a projection of human signatures. Younger. Uninjured. She
selects a clustering method and watches the system sort people into colored
groups.

Not captive.

Building it.

Eli stares.

Anika gives the recording sound.

"The false-positive rate is acceptable if the intervention remains voluntary,"
Maren says. "We are identifying people who need shielding before overload, not
assigning legal identity."

Someone beyond frame asks what prevents later use.

"Governance."

The word from his mother's mouth hurts more than a confession.

The recording jumps years.

Maren is older, wired at the temple, correcting a field model.

"If you collapse the divergence, bay four arrests," she says. "Leave the phase
error. I don't care what the central trace looks like. Those are people, not
noise."

Voluntary correction.

Keeping captives alive inside the apparatus she helped make possible.

Neither innocence nor betrayal. Conduct under a door she built before somebody
locked it.

The transfer reaches nine percent.

Wren says, "Thirty seconds."

Eli pulls the copy cable from the case.

The copy breaks.

The prison lock loses its clock.

Hassan's door opens.

The broken bar sits at nine percent. He does not look at it again.

"Move," Eli says.

Hassan steps into the corridor and nearly falls.

Eli's hand is out before his mind catches up to it — the same reach, the same angle, as a rooftop in Malacca where this man's fist wouldn't open around a ring worth more than his own freedom. The van doors closed on both of them that day. These doors are opening instead.

Sora takes his weight only after he nods.

The guards reach the inner arch.

Eli pockets the recorder containing nine percent of his mother.

They run.

---

"Years ago. Then in a transfer room through glass. She recognized the

The band is too wide for his finger and too heavy to put away. He holds it in

Hassan's eyes open.

Eli closes his hand around the ring.
